========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Um… I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that I’ve been making these really stupid technical mistakes at work — stupid in the sense that I absolutely know better, not that they’re small, because sometimes they’re not small.

I work in video, camera/editing stuff, and lately I’ll do things like label camera cards wrong, or miss a timecode note that I was supposed to catch, or export a client cut and then realize the lower-third is the wrong one, even after I swear I checked it. Like, I’ll have gone through the checklist, or at least I think I have, and then somehow this obvious thing slips through. And it’s embarrassing because these are not beginner mistakes. I’ve been doing this long enough that I should not be sending out a cut with the wrong graphic on it.

It’s gotten to the point where I feel kind of scared of my own brain at work, if that makes sense. Like I’ll finish something and instead of feeling done, I’m thinking, “Okay, what did I miss this time?” And that’s really what brought me in.

Um… I don’t think so? Not in a clear way, anyway. I mean, I still like the things I like. If I’m watching something really well-shot or I’m messing around with an edit that’s actually interesting, I can still get into it. And outside of work, I don’t feel like everything has gone flat or pointless or anything like that.

I guess there are times where I’m too drained or annoyed with myself to enjoy something in the moment, but that feels more like I’m preoccupied than like I’ve stopped caring about it. Like, if I’m thinking about whether I messed something up, then yeah, it’s hard to relax and enjoy a show or dinner or whatever. But I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things I used to enjoy. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying “a lot more than usual,” exactly. I mean, I do worry, but it feels kind of tied to the work mistakes I mentioned, not like my brain has started inventing a whole new list of fears out of nowhere.

Like, I’ll worry after I send something out, or before a handoff, because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something obvious?” But I don’t know that I’m worrying about random things I never used to care about. It’s more that the same work-related stuff has gotten louder because I’ve had enough slip-ups that now I don’t fully trust myself.

So… maybe some increased worry around that, yes, but not in a broad, everything-feels-dangerous kind of way. I haven’t really noticed myself worrying about things that wouldn’t have bothered me before.

I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I definitely replay things in my head after I realize I made a mistake, like, “How did I not catch that?” and I’ll sort of mentally walk back through the steps. But it feels more like trying to figure it out or being frustrated with myself than like an image that just barges into my mind.

I don’t really have, like, vivid scenes of disaster or something bad happening that I can’t get out of my head. It’s more… I’ll remember the moment I spotted the wrong lower-third, or I’ll think about the email where I had to say, “Sorry, sending an updated file,” and I cringe. But I don’t know if I’d call that intrusive, exactly. It’s unpleasant, but it’s tied to the situation.

So, no, I haven’t noticed upsetting thoughts or mental images coming in uninvited in that kind of way. More just overthinking or second-guessing after the fact.

Yeah. I mean, that’s honestly the thing that finally scared me enough to come in, because it’s not just, like, “Oh, I’m a little distracted.” It’s that I’m making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.

Like, I’ll label camera cards wrong, or I’ll miss a timecode note that was right there, or I’ll export a client cut and somehow the wrong lower-third is still in it even after I swear I checked it. I know the workflow, I know what to look for, and I’ll be trying to pay attention, not just rushing through on purpose.

But then something slips anyway, and afterward I’m sitting there thinking, “How did I not see that?” It’s embarrassing, and it makes me feel like I can’t fully trust my own checking anymore. So, yeah, staying focused on the details has definitely been a problem.

I mean, I definitely replay things in my head after I realize I made a mistake, like, “How did I not catch that?” and I’ll sort of mentally walk back through the steps. But it feels more like trying to figure it out or being frustrated with myself than like an image that just barges into my mind.\n\nI don’t really have, like, vivid scenes of disaster or something bad happening that I can’t get out of my head. It’s more… I’ll remember the moment I spotted the wrong lower-third, or I’ll think about the email where I had to say, “Sorry, sending an updated file,” and I cringe. But I don’t know if I’d call that intrusive, exactly. It’s unpleasant, but it’s tied to the situation.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed upsetting thoughts or mental images coming in uninvited in that kind of way. More just overthinking or second-guessing after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, that’s honestly the thing that finally scared me enough to come in, because it’s not just, like, “Oh, I’m a little distracted.” It’s that I’m making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.\n\nLike, I’ll label camera cards wrong, or I’ll miss a timecode note that was right there, or I’ll export a client cut and somehow the wrong lower-third is still in it even after I swear I checked it. And that’s the part that makes me feel kind of crazy, because I’m not new at this. These are basic things in my work. I know the workflow, I know what to look for, and I’ll be trying to pay attention, not just rushing through on purpose.\n\nBut then something slips anyway, and afterward I’m sitting there thinking, “How did I not see that?” It’s embarrassing, and it makes me feel like I can’t fully trust my own checking anymore. So, yeah, staying focused on the details has definitely been a problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the time lately, even when things aren’t going wrong at work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. But I don’t know if I’d call that intrusive, exactly. It’s unpleasant, but it’s tied to the situation.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed upsetting thoughts or mental images coming in uninvited in that kind of way. More just overthinking or second-guessing after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, that’s honestly the thing that finally scared me enough to come in, because it’s not just, like, “Oh, I’m a little distracted.” It’s that I’m making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.\n\nLike, I’ll label camera cards wrong, or I’ll miss a timecode note that was right there, or I’ll export a client cut and somehow the wrong lower-third is still in it even after I swear I checked it. And that’s the part that makes me feel kind of crazy, because I’m not new at this. These are basic things in my work. I know the workflow, I know what to look for, and I’ll be trying to pay attention, not just rushing through on purpose.\n\nBut then something slips anyway, and afterward I’m sitting there thinking, “How did I not see that?” It’s embarrassing, and it makes me feel like I can’t fully trust my own checking anymore. So, yeah, staying focused on the details has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the time lately, even when things aren’t going wrong at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “most of the time,” no. I mean, I’ve felt pretty discouraged and embarrassed when something goes wrong at work, and sometimes that mood kind of follows me for a while afterward because I keep thinking about it. But it feels connected to the mistakes, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen nothing is actively going wrong, I don’t feel hopeless in this constant background way. I’m not walking around feeling sad all day for no reason. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself, or I feel this drop in my stomach when I realize I missed something, and then I can be kind of down about it because it makes me question myself.\n\nBut outside of that, no, I haven’t really noticed being sad or hopeless most of the time. It’s more situational than constant.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down than usual, even when you haven't been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Not really, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially if I’ve had a long workday or I’ve been staring at footage and screens for hours, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling unusually run down when nothing demanding is happening.

It’s not like I’m waking up already exhausted for no reason, or like I can’t get through normal stuff because my body feels heavy or drained. So… no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing for me.

noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’m not walking around feeling sad all day for no reason. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself, or I feel this drop in my stomach when I realize I missed something, and then I can be kind of down about it because it makes me question myself.\n\nBut outside of that, no, I haven’t really noticed being sad or hopeless most of the time. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah — definitely the restless side, not the moving-slowly side. I don’t think anyone would describe me as slowed down, honestly. It’s more like… sitting still can feel weirdly uncomfortable, like my body is arguing with the situation.

The clearest example is client screenings. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and watch quietly, take in the cut, pay attention to their reactions, all of that. But after a while it feels like there’s this pressure building under my skin, like I need to get up or shift around or do something with my body. And I’m trying to look normal, so I’ll cross and uncross my legs, adjust how I’m sitting, mess with a pen, whatever tiny thing I can do that doesn’t make it obvious.

It’s not that I’m bored exactly, or that I don’t care. Sometimes I really care, which almost makes it worse because I’m trying so hard to stay put and be professional. But internally I’m just like, “Can we pause for one second, can I stand up, can I walk to the back of the room?” It feels like holding something in. So yes, restless is a good word for it.

Like I’ll finish something and instead of feeling done, I’m thinking, “Okay, what did I miss this time?” And that’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of things that you used to like doing lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a clear way, anyway. I mean, I still like the things I like. If I’m watching something really well-shot or I’m messing around with an edit that’s actually interesting, I can still get into it. And outside of work, I don’t feel like everything has gone flat or pointless or anything like that.\n\nI guess there are times where I’m too drained or annoyed with myself to enjoy something in the moment, but that feels more like I’m preoccupied than like I’ve stopped caring about it. I don’t think so, not in that kind of sudden, out-of-nowhere way. So… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least not the way you’re describing it.

It’s more that the same work-related stuff has gotten louder because I’ve had enough slip-ups that now I don’t fully trust myself.\n\nSo… maybe some increased worry around that, yes, but not in a broad, everything-feels-dangerous kind of way. I haven’t really noticed myself worrying about things that wouldn’t have bothered me before.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having upsetting thoughts or mental images that come into your mind uninvited, like seeing the mistake happen again or imagining something bad resulting from it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I definitely replay things in my head after I realize I made a mistake, like, “How did I not catch that?” and I’ll sort of mentally walk back through the steps. But it feels more like trying to figure it out or being frustrated with myself than like an image that just barges into my mind.\n\nI don’t really have, like, vivid scenes of disaster or something bad happening that I can’t get out of my head. It’s more… I’ll remember the moment I spotted the wrong lower-third, or I’ll think about the email where I had to say, “Sorry, sending an updated file,” and I cringe. But I don’t know if I’d call that intrusive, exactly. It’s unpleasant, but it’s tied to the situation.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed upsetting thoughts or mental images coming in uninvited in that kind of way. More just overthinking or second-guessing after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, that’s honestly the thing that finally scared me enough to come in, because it’s not just, like, “Oh, I’m a little distracted.” It’s that I’m making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.\n\nLike, I’ll label camera cards wrong, or I’ll miss a timecode note that was right there, or I’ll export a client cut and somehow the wrong lower-third is still in it even after I swear I checked it. And that’s the part that makes me feel kind of crazy, because I’m not new at this. These are basic things in my work. I know the workflow, I know what to look for, and I’ll be trying to pay attention, not just rushing through on purpose.\n\nBut then something slips anyway, and afterward I’m sitting there thinking, “How did I not see that?” It’s embarrassing, and it makes me feel like I can’t fully trust my own checking anymore. So, yeah, staying focused on the details has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the time lately, even when things aren’t going wrong at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “most of the time,” no. I mean, I’ve felt pretty discouraged and embarrassed when something goes wrong at work, and sometimes that mood kind of follows me for a while afterward because I keep thinking about it. But it feels connected to the mistakes, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen nothing is actively going wrong, I don’t feel hopeless in this constant background way. I’m not walking around feeling sad all day for no reason. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself, or I feel this drop in my stomach when I realize I missed something, and then I can be kind of down about it because it makes me question myself.\n\nBut outside of that, no, I haven’t really noticed being sad or hopeless most of the time. It’s more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down than usual, even when you haven't been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially if I’ve had a long workday or I’ve been staring at footage and screens for hours, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling unusually run down when nothing demanding is happening.\n\nIt’s not like I’m waking up already exhausted for no reason, or like I can’t get through normal stuff because my body feels heavy or drained. If I feel wiped out, I can usually point to why — a long day, stress after something went wrong, or just too much time focusing on edits. But I haven’t noticed this separate, constant tiredness that seems out of proportion.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or unable to sit still lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah — definitely the restless side, not the moving-slowly side. I don’t think anyone would describe me as slowed down, honestly. It’s more like… sitting still can feel weirdly uncomfortable, like my body is arguing with the situation.\n\nThe clearest example is client screenings. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and watch quietly, take in the cut, pay attention to their reactions, all of that. But after a while it feels like there’s this pressure building under my skin, like I need to get up or shift around or do something with my body. And I’m trying to look normal, so I’ll cross and uncross my legs, adjust how I’m sitting, mess with a pen, whatever tiny thing I can do that doesn’t make it obvious.\n\nIt’s not that I’m bored exactly, or that I don’t care. Sometimes I really care, which almost makes it worse because I’m trying so hard to stay put and be professional. But internally I’m just like, “Can we pause for one second, can I stand up, can I walk to the back of the room?” It feels like holding something in. So yes, restless is a good word for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort, like your heart is racing, you're short of breath, or you feel like something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that kind of sudden, out-of-nowhere way. I mean, I definitely get tense or worked up when something stressful is happening, and I can feel that in my body sometimes, but I wouldn’t describe it as an episode where I suddenly feel terrified or like I can’t breathe or something terrible is about to happen.\n\nLike, if I’m upset about something, there’s usually a pretty clear reason, and it feels more like stress or embarrassment or adrenaline, not this wave of fear that comes out of nowhere. I haven’t had moments where I’m thinking, “Am I having a heart attack?” or where I feel like I’m losing control.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least not the way you’re describing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Hmm… I don’t think so. Not really in the sense of things feeling flat or like I can’t enjoy them at all.

I mean, I definitely have days where I’m distracted or preoccupied, and if there’s something annoying hanging over me, it can make it harder to fully relax into something fun. Sometimes I really care, which almost makes it worse because I’m trying so hard to stay put and be professional. I mean, I definitely get tense or worked up when something stressful is happening, and I can feel that in my body sometimes, but I wouldn’t describe it as an episode where I suddenly feel terrified or like I can’t breathe or something terrible is about to happen.\n\nLike, if I’m upset about something, there’s usually a pretty clear reason, and it feels more like stress or embarrassment or adrenaline, not this wave of fear that comes out of nowhere. Not really in the sense of things feeling flat or like I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nI mean, I definitely have days where I’m distracted or preoccupied, and if there’s something annoying hanging over me, it can make it harder to fully relax into something fun. But if I’m not stuck thinking about work mistakes or some specific thing I’m upset about, I don’t usually have this thing where I look at something I normally enjoy and feel nothing.\n\nLike if I’m watching something I like, or doing something low-key that’s usually fun for me, I can still get into it. I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just… not wanting to do the task, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I feel mentally drained in a heavy way when I’m otherwise rested, like there’s absolutely no drive there. It’s more that certain focus-heavy tasks can feel hard to get into, especially reviewing something carefully or doing the kind of editing pass where I have to be very precise and not miss anything.

But if you’re asking whether I have this across-the-board lack of mental energy, even when I’m not tired, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. I can usually find energy if there’s enough urgency or if something grabs me. It’s just that some tasks feel like they take a ridiculous amount of effort to start and stay with, and I can sit there sort of circling the work before actually doing it.

So… maybe a little, depending on what you mean, but I don’t think it feels like low drive exactly. More like friction. Like my brain doesn’t want to lock onto the thing unless there’s pressure or momentum.

I haven’t had moments where I’m thinking, “Am I having a heart attack?” or where I feel like I’m losing control.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. But if I’m not stuck thinking about work mistakes or some specific thing I’m upset about, I don’t usually have this thing where I look at something I normally enjoy and feel nothing.\n\nLike if I’m watching something I like, or doing something low-key that’s usually fun for me, I can still get into it. Sometimes it takes me a bit to settle down and actually focus on it, but once I’m there, I can enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed whole days where pleasure is just gone for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not physically tired, it feels like you don’t have the mental energy or drive to push through tasks that require focus, like editing or reviewing your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just… not wanting to do the task, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I feel mentally drained in a heavy way when I’m otherwise rested, like there’s absolutely no drive there. It’s more that certain focus-heavy tasks can feel hard to get into, especially reviewing something carefully or doing the kind of editing pass where I have to be very precise and not miss anything.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I have this across-the-board lack of mental energy, even when I’m not tired, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. I can usually find energy if there’s enough urgency or if something grabs me. It’s just that some tasks feel like they take a ridiculous amount of effort to start and stay with, and I can sit there sort of circling the work before actually doing it.\n\nSo… maybe a little, depending on what you mean, but I don’t think it feels like low drive exactly. More like friction. Like my brain doesn’t want to lock onto the thing unless there’s pressure or momentum.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any days where you feel like your thoughts are foggy, it's hard to make decisions, or you can't think clearly even when you're not distracted by work mistakes?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d call it foggy exactly. I mean, I have moments where I’m trying to think through something and it feels like there are too many little pieces moving around, and then I kind of get stuck choosing where to start or what order to do things in. But that feels more like I’m scattered or I’m overcomplicating it, not like my brain is cloudy or I can’t think clearly.

Decision-making can be annoying for me sometimes, especially if it’s a bunch of small choices that all seem equally important, but I usually can make the decision once I force myself to slow down. It’s not like I have whole days where I’m just mentally blank or confused.

And if I’m not distracted by a specific mistake or something stressful, I generally feel like I can think fine. Maybe not always in the most organized way, but clear enough. So… no, I haven’t really noticed days of fogginess like that.

Like, I’ll have gone through the checklist, or at least I think I have, and then somehow this obvious thing slips through. And it’s embarrassing because these are not beginner mistakes. I’ve been doing this long enough that I should not be sending out a cut with the wrong graphic on it.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where I feel kind of scared of my own brain at work, if that makes sense. Like I’ll finish something and instead of feeling done, I’m thinking, “Okay, what did I miss this time?” And that’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of things that you used to like doing lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a clear way, anyway. I mean, I still like the things I like. If I’m watching something really well-shot or I’m messing around with an edit that’s actually interesting, I can still get into it. And outside of work, I don’t feel like everything has gone flat or pointless or anything like that.\n\nI guess there are times where I’m too drained or annoyed with myself to enjoy something in the moment, but that feels more like I’m preoccupied than like I’ve stopped caring about it. Like, if I’m thinking about whether I messed something up, then yeah, it’s hard to relax and enjoy a show or dinner or whatever. But I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things I used to enjoy. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot more than usual, even about things that you wouldn't have worried about before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying “a lot more than usual,” exactly. I mean, I do worry, but it feels kind of tied to the work mistakes I mentioned, not like my brain has started inventing a whole new list of fears out of nowhere.\n\nLike, I’ll worry after I send something out, or before a handoff, because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something obvious?” But I don’t know that I’m worrying about random things I never used to care about. It’s more that the same work-related stuff has gotten louder because I’ve had enough slip-ups that now I don’t fully trust myself.\n\nSo… maybe some increased worry around that, yes, but not in a broad, everything-feels-dangerous kind of way. I haven’t really noticed myself worrying about things that wouldn’t have bothered me before.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having upsetting thoughts or mental images that come into your mind uninvited, like seeing the mistake happen again or imagining something bad resulting from it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. But it feels more like trying to figure it out or being frustrated with myself than like an image that just barges into my mind.\n\nI don’t really have, like, vivid scenes of disaster or something bad happening that I can’t get out of my head. It’s more… I’ll remember the moment I spotted the wrong lower-third, or I’ll think about the email where I had to say, “Sorry, sending an updated file,” and I cringe. But I don’t know if I’d call that intrusive, exactly. It’s unpleasant, but it’s tied to the situation.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed upsetting thoughts or mental images coming in uninvited in that kind of way. More just overthinking or second-guessing after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, that’s honestly the thing that finally scared me enough to come in, because it’s not just, like, “Oh, I’m a little distracted.” It’s that I’m making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.\n\nLike, I’ll label camera cards wrong, or I’ll miss a timecode note that was right there, or I’ll export a client cut and somehow the wrong lower-third is still in it even after I swear I checked it. And that’s the part that makes me feel kind of crazy, because I’m not new at this. These are basic things in my work. I know the workflow, I know what to look for, and I’ll be trying to pay attention, not just rushing through on purpose.\n\nBut then something slips anyway, and afterward I’m sitting there thinking, “How did I not see that?” It’s embarrassing, and it makes me feel like I can’t fully trust my own checking anymore. So, yeah, staying focused on the details has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the time lately, even when things aren’t going wrong at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “most of the time,” no. I mean, I’ve felt pretty discouraged and embarrassed when something goes wrong at work, and sometimes that mood kind of follows me for a while afterward because I keep thinking about it. But it feels connected to the mistakes, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen nothing is actively going wrong, I don’t feel hopeless in this constant background way. I’m not walking around feeling sad all day for no reason. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself, or I feel this drop in my stomach when I realize I missed something, and then I can be kind of down about it because it makes me question myself.\n\nBut outside of that, no, I haven’t really noticed being sad or hopeless most of the time. It’s more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down than usual, even when you haven't been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially if I’ve had a long workday or I’ve been staring at footage and screens for hours, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling unusually run down when nothing demanding is happening.\n\nIt’s not like I’m waking up already exhausted for no reason, or like I can’t get through normal stuff because my body feels heavy or drained. If I feel wiped out, I can usually point to why — a long day, stress after something went wrong, or just too much time focusing on edits. But I haven’t noticed this separate, constant tiredness that seems out of proportion.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or unable to sit still lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah — definitely the restless side, not the moving-slowly side. I don’t think anyone would describe me as slowed down, honestly. It’s more like… sitting still can feel weirdly uncomfortable, like my body is arguing with the situation.\n\nThe clearest example is client screenings. But after a while it feels like there’s this pressure building under my skin, like I need to get up or shift around or do something with my body. And I’m trying to look normal, so I’ll cross and uncross my legs, adjust how I’m sitting, mess with a pen, whatever tiny thing I can do that doesn’t make it obvious.\n\nIt’s not that I’m bored exactly, or that I don’t care. Sometimes I really care, which almost makes it worse because I’m trying so hard to stay put and be professional. But internally I’m just like, “Can we pause for one second, can I stand up, can I walk to the back of the room?” It feels like holding something in. But it feels connected to the mistakes, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen nothing is actively going wrong, I don’t feel hopeless in this constant background way. I’m not walking around feeling sad all day for no reason. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself, or I feel this drop in my stomach when I realize I missed something, and then I can be kind of down about it because it makes me question myself.\n\nBut outside of that, no, I haven’t really noticed being sad or hopeless most of the time. It’s more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down than usual, even when you haven't been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially if I’ve had a long workday or I’ve been staring at footage and screens for hours, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling unusually run down when nothing demanding is happening.\n\nIt’s not like I’m waking up already exhausted for no reason, or like I can’t get through normal stuff because my body feels heavy or drained. If I feel wiped out, I can usually point to why — a long day, stress after something went wrong, or just too much time focusing on edits. But I haven’t noticed this separate, constant tiredness that seems out of proportion.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or unable to sit still lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah — definitely the restless side, not the moving-slowly side. I don’t think anyone would describe me as slowed down, honestly. It’s more like… sitting still can feel weirdly uncomfortable, like my body is arguing with the situation.\n\nThe clearest example is client screenings. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and watch quietly, take in the cut, pay attention to their reactions, all of that. But after a while it feels like there’s this pressure building under my skin, like I need to get up or shift around or do something with my body. And I’m trying to look normal, so I’ll cross and uncross my legs, adjust how I’m sitting, mess with a pen, whatever tiny thing I can do that doesn’t make it obvious.\n\nIt’s not that I’m bored exactly, or that I don’t care. Sometimes I really care, which almost makes it worse because I’m trying so hard to stay put and be professional. But internally I’m just like, “Can we pause for one second, can I stand up, can I walk to the back of the room?” It feels like holding something in. So yes, restless is a good word for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort, like your heart is racing, you're short of breath, or you feel like something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that kind of sudden, out-of-nowhere way. I mean, I definitely get tense or worked up when something stressful is happening, and I can feel that in my body sometimes, but I wouldn’t describe it as an episode where I suddenly feel terrified or like I can’t breathe or something terrible is about to happen.\n\nLike, if I’m upset about something, there’s usually a pretty clear reason, and it feels more like stress or embarrassment or adrenaline, not this wave of fear that comes out of nowhere. I haven’t had moments where I’m thinking, “Am I having a heart attack?” or where I feel like I’m losing control.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least not the way you’re describing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you normally enjoy, even if you’re not thinking about work mistakes at the time?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t think so. Not really in the sense of things feeling flat or like I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nI mean, I definitely have days where I’m distracted or preoccupied, and if there’s something annoying hanging over me, it can make it harder to fully relax into something fun. But if I’m not stuck thinking about work mistakes or some specific thing I’m upset about, I don’t usually have this thing where I look at something I normally enjoy and feel nothing.\n\nLike if I’m watching something I like, or doing something low-key that’s usually fun for me, I can still get into it. Sometimes it takes me a bit to settle down and actually focus on it, but once I’m there, I can enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed whole days where pleasure is just gone for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not physically tired, it feels like you don’t have the mental energy or drive to push through tasks that require focus, like editing or reviewing your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just… not wanting to do the task, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I feel mentally drained in a heavy way when I’m otherwise rested, like there’s absolutely no drive there. It’s more that certain focus-heavy tasks can feel hard to get into, especially reviewing something carefully or doing the kind of editing pass where I have to be very precise and not miss anything.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I have this across-the-board lack of mental energy, even when I’m not tired, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. I can usually find energy if there’s enough urgency or if something grabs me. It’s just that some tasks feel like they take a ridiculous amount of effort to start and stay with, and I can sit there sort of circling the work before actually doing it.\n\nSo… maybe a little, depending on what you mean, but I don’t think it feels like low drive exactly. More like friction. Like my brain doesn’t want to lock onto the thing unless there’s pressure or momentum.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel like your thoughts are foggy, it's hard to make decisions, or you can't think clearly even when you're not distracted by work mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it foggy exactly. I mean, I have moments where I’m trying to think through something and it feels like there are too many little pieces moving around, and then I kind of get stuck choosing where to start or what order to do things in. But that feels more like I’m scattered or I’m overcomplicating it, not like my brain is cloudy or I can’t think clearly.\n\nDecision-making can be annoying for me sometimes, especially if it’s a bunch of small choices that all seem equally important, but I usually can make the decision once I force myself to slow down. It’s not like I have whole days where I’m just mentally blank or confused.\n\nAnd if I’m not distracted by a specific mistake or something stressful, I generally feel like I can think fine. Maybe not always in the most organized way, but clear enough. So… no, I haven’t really noticed days of fogginess like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think so, not in that specific way. I mean, I definitely have times where getting myself to start anything feels weirdly hard, even if it’s something I know I usually like. But once I actually start, or once I’m kind of pulled into it, I can still enjoy it.

So if you mean, like, I wake up and nothing sounds appealing all day and I don’t want to do any of the things I normally enjoy, even with work stuff not on my mind… I don’t really recognize that. It’s more that I might procrastinate on even fun things, or I’ll bounce around and not settle on what to do, and then later I’m annoyed because I wasted the free time.

But it doesn’t feel like the motivation is gone because I’m emotionally flat or because I don’t care about anything. It’s more like I have trouble shifting into the activity. If someone else gets it started, or there’s a plan already, I’m usually fine. So no, I haven’t really noticed whole days like that.

It’s more like I get frustrated with myself, or I feel this drop in my stomach when I realize I missed something, and then I can be kind of down about it because it makes me question myself.\n\nBut outside of that, no, I haven’t really noticed being sad or hopeless most of the time. It’s more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down than usual, even when you haven't been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially if I’ve had a long workday or I’ve been staring at footage and screens for hours, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling unusually run down when nothing demanding is happening.\n\nIt’s not like I’m waking up already exhausted for no reason, or like I can’t get through normal stuff because my body feels heavy or drained. If I feel wiped out, I can usually point to why — a long day, stress after something went wrong, or just too much time focusing on edits. But I haven’t noticed this separate, constant tiredness that seems out of proportion.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or unable to sit still lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah — definitely the restless side, not the moving-slowly side. I don’t think anyone would describe me as slowed down, honestly. It’s more like… sitting still can feel weirdly uncomfortable, like my body is arguing with the situation.\n\nThe clearest example is client screenings. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and watch quietly, take in the cut, pay attention to their reactions, all of that. But after a while it feels like there’s this pressure building under my skin, like I need to get up or shift around or do something with my body. And I’m trying to look normal, so I’ll cross and uncross my legs, adjust how I’m sitting, mess with a pen, whatever tiny thing I can do that doesn’t make it obvious.\n\nIt’s not that I’m bored exactly, or that I don’t care. Sometimes I really care, which almost makes it worse because I’m trying so hard to stay put and be professional. But internally I’m just like, “Can we pause for one second, can I stand up, can I walk to the back of the room?” It feels like holding something in. So yes, restless is a good word for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort, like your heart is racing, you're short of breath, or you feel like something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that kind of sudden, out-of-nowhere way. I mean, I definitely get tense or worked up when something stressful is happening, and I can feel that in my body sometimes, but I wouldn’t describe it as an episode where I suddenly feel terrified or like I can’t breathe or something terrible is about to happen.\n\nLike, if I’m upset about something, there’s usually a pretty clear reason, and it feels more like stress or embarrassment or adrenaline, not this wave of fear that comes out of nowhere. I haven’t had moments where I’m thinking, “Am I having a heart attack?” or where I feel like I’m losing control.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least not the way you’re describing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you normally enjoy, even if you’re not thinking about work mistakes at the time?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t think so. Not really in the sense of things feeling flat or like I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nI mean, I definitely have days where I’m distracted or preoccupied, and if there’s something annoying hanging over me, it can make it harder to fully relax into something fun. But if I’m not stuck thinking about work mistakes or some specific thing I’m upset about, I don’t usually have this thing where I look at something I normally enjoy and feel nothing.\n\nLike if I’m watching something I like, or doing something low-key that’s usually fun for me, I can still get into it. Sometimes it takes me a bit to settle down and actually focus on it, but once I’m there, I can enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed whole days where pleasure is just gone for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not physically tired, it feels like you don’t have the mental energy or drive to push through tasks that require focus, like editing or reviewing your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just… not wanting to do the task, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I feel mentally drained in a heavy way when I’m otherwise rested, like there’s absolutely no drive there. It’s more that certain focus-heavy tasks can feel hard to get into, especially reviewing something carefully or doing the kind of editing pass where I have to be very precise and not miss anything.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I have this across-the-board lack of mental energy, even when I’m not tired, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. I can usually find energy if there’s enough urgency or if something grabs me. It’s just that some tasks feel like they take a ridiculous amount of effort to start and stay with, and I can sit there sort of circling the work before actually doing it.\n\nSo… maybe a little, depending on what you mean, but I don’t think it feels like low drive exactly. More like friction. Like my brain doesn’t want to lock onto the thing unless there’s pressure or momentum.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel like your thoughts are foggy, it's hard to make decisions, or you can't think clearly even when you're not distracted by work mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it foggy exactly. I mean, I have moments where I’m trying to think through something and it feels like there are too many little pieces moving around, and then I kind of get stuck choosing where to start or what order to do things in. But that feels more like I’m scattered or I’m overcomplicating it, not like my brain is cloudy or I can’t think clearly.\n\nDecision-making can be annoying for me sometimes, especially if it’s a bunch of small choices that all seem equally important, but I usually can make the decision once I force myself to slow down. It’s not like I have whole days where I’m just mentally blank or confused.\n\nAnd if I’m not distracted by a specific mistake or something stressful, I generally feel like I can think fine. Maybe not always in the most organized way, but clear enough. So… no, I haven’t really noticed days of fogginess like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t feel motivated to do things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work or mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that specific way. But once I actually start, or once I’m kind of pulled into it, I can still enjoy it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, I wake up and nothing sounds appealing all day and I don’t want to do any of the things I normally enjoy, even with work stuff not on my mind… I don’t really recognize that. It’s more like I have trouble shifting into the activity. If someone else gets it started, or there’s a plan already, I’m usually fine. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. I mean, I definitely have days where I’m more revved up than other days, or I’m talking a lot, or I feel like I have a bunch of momentum and I’m jumping from thing to thing. But I don’t know that I’d call it “unusually high” in the way you might mean, like a mood state that feels really different from me or that other people are reacting to like, “What is going on with you?”

I can get irritable, sure, but usually there’s some reason I can point to, or it’s more like I’m impatient because things feel too slow or I’m trying to get something done. I don’t think I’ve had stretches where I’m just intensely irritable or euphoric out of nowhere and everyone else is on a totally different wavelength.

And “extremely energetic” is a little hard for me to answer because I can have bursts of energy, especially if something is interesting or there’s a lot happening. But it doesn’t feel like this separate period where my mood is elevated or I’m on top of the world or anything. It’s more just… my normal level can run kind of high sometimes, if that makes sense. So no, I haven’t really noticed periods like that.

I know the workflow, I know what to look for, and I’ll be trying to pay attention, not just rushing through on purpose.\n\nBut then something slips anyway, and afterward I’m sitting there thinking, “How did I not see that?” It’s embarrassing, and it makes me feel like I can’t fully trust my own checking anymore. So, yeah, staying focused on the details has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the time lately, even when things aren’t going wrong at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “most of the time,” no. I mean, I’ve felt pretty discouraged and embarrassed when something goes wrong at work, and sometimes that mood kind of follows me for a while afterward because I keep thinking about it. But it feels connected to the mistakes, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen nothing is actively going wrong, I don’t feel hopeless in this constant background way. I’m not walking around feeling sad all day for no reason. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself, or I feel this drop in my stomach when I realize I missed something, and then I can be kind of down about it because it makes me question myself.\n\nBut outside of that, no, I haven’t really noticed being sad or hopeless most of the time. It’s more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down than usual, even when you haven't been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially if I’ve had a long workday or I’ve been staring at footage and screens for hours, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling unusually run down when nothing demanding is happening.\n\nIt’s not like I’m waking up already exhausted for no reason, or like I can’t get through normal stuff because my body feels heavy or drained. If I feel wiped out, I can usually point to why — a long day, stress after something went wrong, or just too much time focusing on edits. But I haven’t noticed this separate, constant tiredness that seems out of proportion.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or unable to sit still lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah — definitely the restless side, not the moving-slowly side. I don’t think anyone would describe me as slowed down, honestly. It’s more like… sitting still can feel weirdly uncomfortable, like my body is arguing with the situation.\n\nThe clearest example is client screenings. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and watch quietly, take in the cut, pay attention to their reactions, all of that. But after a while it feels like there’s this pressure building under my skin, like I need to get up or shift around or do something with my body. And I’m trying to look normal, so I’ll cross and uncross my legs, adjust how I’m sitting, mess with a pen, whatever tiny thing I can do that doesn’t make it obvious.\n\nIt’s not that I’m bored exactly, or that I don’t care. Sometimes I really care, which almost makes it worse because I’m trying so hard to stay put and be professional. But internally I’m just like, “Can we pause for one second, can I stand up, can I walk to the back of the room?” It feels like holding something in. So yes, restless is a good word for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort, like your heart is racing, you're short of breath, or you feel like something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that kind of sudden, out-of-nowhere way. I mean, I definitely get tense or worked up when something stressful is happening, and I can feel that in my body sometimes, but I wouldn’t describe it as an episode where I suddenly feel terrified or like I can’t breathe or something terrible is about to happen.\n\nLike, if I’m upset about something, there’s usually a pretty clear reason, and it feels more like stress or embarrassment or adrenaline, not this wave of fear that comes out of nowhere. I haven’t had moments where I’m thinking, “Am I having a heart attack?” or where I feel like I’m losing control.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least not the way you’re describing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you normally enjoy, even if you’re not thinking about work mistakes at the time?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t think so. Not really in the sense of things feeling flat or like I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nI mean, I definitely have days where I’m distracted or preoccupied, and if there’s something annoying hanging over me, it can make it harder to fully relax into something fun. But if I’m not stuck thinking about work mistakes or some specific thing I’m upset about, I don’t usually have this thing where I look at something I normally enjoy and feel nothing.\n\nLike if I’m watching something I like, or doing something low-key that’s usually fun for me, I can still get into it. Sometimes it takes me a bit to settle down and actually focus on it, but once I’m there, I can enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed whole days where pleasure is just gone for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not physically tired, it feels like you don’t have the mental energy or drive to push through tasks that require focus, like editing or reviewing your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just… not wanting to do the task, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I feel mentally drained in a heavy way when I’m otherwise rested, like there’s absolutely no drive there. It’s more that certain focus-heavy tasks can feel hard to get into, especially reviewing something carefully or doing the kind of editing pass where I have to be very precise and not miss anything.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I have this across-the-board lack of mental energy, even when I’m not tired, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. I can usually find energy if there’s enough urgency or if something grabs me. It’s just that some tasks feel like they take a ridiculous amount of effort to start and stay with, and I can sit there sort of circling the work before actually doing it.\n\nSo… maybe a little, depending on what you mean, but I don’t think it feels like low drive exactly. More like friction. Like my brain doesn’t want to lock onto the thing unless there’s pressure or momentum.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel like your thoughts are foggy, it's hard to make decisions, or you can't think clearly even when you're not distracted by work mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it foggy exactly. I mean, I have moments where I’m trying to think through something and it feels like there are too many little pieces moving around, and then I kind of get stuck choosing where to start or what order to do things in. But that feels more like I’m scattered or I’m overcomplicating it, not like my brain is cloudy or I can’t think clearly.\n\nDecision-making can be annoying for me sometimes, especially if it’s a bunch of small choices that all seem equally important, but I usually can make the decision once I force myself to slow down. It’s not like I have whole days where I’m just mentally blank or confused.\n\nAnd if I’m not distracted by a specific mistake or something stressful, I generally feel like I can think fine. Maybe not always in the most organized way, but clear enough. So… no, I haven’t really noticed days of fogginess like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t feel motivated to do things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work or mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that specific way. I mean, I definitely have times where getting myself to start anything feels weirdly hard, even if it’s something I know I usually like. But once I actually start, or once I’m kind of pulled into it, I can still enjoy it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, I wake up and nothing sounds appealing all day and I don’t want to do any of the things I normally enjoy, even with work stuff not on my mind… I don’t really recognize that. It’s more that I might procrastinate on even fun things, or I’ll bounce around and not settle on what to do, and then later I’m annoyed because I wasted the free time.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like the motivation is gone because I’m emotionally flat or because I don’t care about anything. It’s more like I have trouble shifting into the activity. If someone else gets it started, or there’s a plan already, I’m usually fine. So no, I haven’t really noticed whole days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time where your mood was unusually high, irritable, or you felt extremely energetic, even if others around you didn't seem to share that mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I definitely have days where I’m more revved up than other days, or I’m talking a lot, or I feel like I have a bunch of momentum and I’m jumping from thing to thing. But I don’t know that I’d call it “unusually high” in the way you might mean, like a mood state that feels really different from me or that other people are reacting to like, “What is going on with you?”\n\nI can get irritable, sure, but usually there’s some reason I can point to, or it’s more like I’m impatient because things feel too slow or I’m trying to get something done. I don’t think I’ve had stretches where I’m just intensely irritable or euphoric out of nowhere and everyone else is on a totally different wavelength.\n\nAnd “extremely energetic” is a little hard for me to answer because I can have bursts of energy, especially if something is interesting or there’s a lot happening. Like, if I’m thinking about whether I messed something up, then yeah, it’s hard to relax and enjoy a show or dinner or whatever. But I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things I used to enjoy. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot more than usual, even about things that you wouldn't have worried about before?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying “a lot more than usual,” exactly. I mean, I do worry, but it feels kind of tied to the work mistakes I mentioned, not like my brain has started inventing a whole new list of fears out of nowhere.\n\nLike, I’ll worry after I send something out, or before a handoff, because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something obvious?” But I don’t know that I’m worrying about random things I never used to care about. It’s more that the same work-related stuff has gotten louder because I’ve had enough slip-ups that now I don’t fully trust myself.\n\nSo… maybe some increased worry around that, yes, but not in a broad, everything-feels-dangerous kind of way. I haven’t really noticed myself worrying about things that wouldn’t have bothered me before.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having upsetting thoughts or mental images that come into your mind uninvited, like seeing the mistake happen again or imagining something bad resulting from it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I definitely replay things in my head after I realize I made a mistake, like, “How did I not catch that?” and I’ll sort of mentally walk back through the steps. But it feels more like trying to figure it out or being frustrated with myself than like an image that just barges into my mind.\n\nI don’t really have, like, vivid scenes of disaster or something bad happening that I can’t get out of my head. It’s more… I’ll remember the moment I spotted the wrong lower-third, or I’ll think about the email where I had to say, “Sorry, sending an updated file,” and I cringe. But I don’t know if I’d call that intrusive, exactly. It’s unpleasant, but it’s tied to the situation.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed upsetting thoughts or mental images coming in uninvited in that kind of way. More just overthinking or second-guessing after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks, even when you're trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, that’s honestly the thing that finally scared me enough to come in, because it’s not just, like, “Oh, I’m a little distracted.” It’s that I’m making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.\n\nLike, I’ll label camera cards wrong, or I’ll miss a timecode note that was right there, or I’ll export a client cut and somehow the wrong lower-third is still in it even after I swear I checked it. And that’s the part that makes me feel kind of crazy, because I’m not new at this. These are basic things in my work. I know the workflow, I know what to look for, and I’ll be trying to pay attention, not just rushing through on purpose.\n\nBut then something slips anyway, and afterward I’m sitting there thinking, “How did I not see that?” It’s embarrassing, and it makes me feel like I can’t fully trust my own checking anymore. So, yeah, staying focused on the details has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the time lately, even when things aren’t going wrong at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “most of the time,” no. I mean, I’ve felt pretty discouraged and embarrassed when something goes wrong at work, and sometimes that mood kind of follows me for a while afterward because I keep thinking about it. But it feels connected to the mistakes, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen nothing is actively going wrong, I don’t feel hopeless in this constant background way. I’m not walking around feeling sad all day for no reason. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself, or I feel this drop in my stomach when I realize I missed something, and then I can be kind of down about it because it makes me question myself.\n\nBut outside of that, no, I haven’t really noticed being sad or hopeless most of the time. It’s more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down than usual, even when you haven't been doing anything particularly demanding?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially if I’ve had a long workday or I’ve been staring at footage and screens for hours, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling unusually run down when nothing demanding is happening.\n\nIt’s not like I’m waking up already exhausted for no reason, or like I can’t get through normal stuff because my body feels heavy or drained. If I feel wiped out, I can usually point to why — a long day, stress after something went wrong, or just too much time focusing on edits. But I haven’t noticed this separate, constant tiredness that seems out of proportion.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or unable to sit still lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah — definitely the restless side, not the moving-slowly side. I don’t think anyone would describe me as slowed down, honestly. It’s more like… sitting still can feel weirdly uncomfortable, like my body is arguing with the situation.\n\nThe clearest example is client screenings. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and watch quietly, take in the cut, pay attention to their reactions, all of that. But after a while it feels like there’s this pressure building under my skin, like I need to get up or shift around or do something with my body. And I’m trying to look normal, so I’ll cross and uncross my legs, adjust how I’m sitting, mess with a pen, whatever tiny thing I can do that doesn’t make it obvious.\n\nIt’s not that I’m bored exactly, or that I don’t care. Sometimes I really care, which almost makes it worse because I’m trying so hard to stay put and be professional. But internally I’m just like, “Can we pause for one second, can I stand up, can I walk to the back of the room?” It feels like holding something in. So yes, restless is a good word for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort, like your heart is racing, you're short of breath, or you feel like something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that kind of sudden, out-of-nowhere way. I mean, I definitely get tense or worked up when something stressful is happening, and I can feel that in my body sometimes, but I wouldn’t describe it as an episode where I suddenly feel terrified or like I can’t breathe or something terrible is about to happen.\n\nLike, if I’m upset about something, there’s usually a pretty clear reason, and it feels more like stress or embarrassment or adrenaline, not this wave of fear that comes out of nowhere. I haven’t had moments where I’m thinking, “Am I having a heart attack?” or where I feel like I’m losing control.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least not the way you’re describing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t take pleasure in things you normally enjoy, even if you’re not thinking about work mistakes at the time?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t think so. Not really in the sense of things feeling flat or like I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nI mean, I definitely have days where I’m distracted or preoccupied, and if there’s something annoying hanging over me, it can make it harder to fully relax into something fun. But if I’m not stuck thinking about work mistakes or some specific thing I’m upset about, I don’t usually have this thing where I look at something I normally enjoy and feel nothing.\n\nLike if I’m watching something I like, or doing something low-key that’s usually fun for me, I can still get into it. Sometimes it takes me a bit to settle down and actually focus on it, but once I’m there, I can enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed whole days where pleasure is just gone for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not physically tired, it feels like you don’t have the mental energy or drive to push through tasks that require focus, like editing or reviewing your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just… not wanting to do the task, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I feel mentally drained in a heavy way when I’m otherwise rested, like there’s absolutely no drive there. It’s more that certain focus-heavy tasks can feel hard to get into, especially reviewing something carefully or doing the kind of editing pass where I have to be very precise and not miss anything.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I have this across-the-board lack of mental energy, even when I’m not tired, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. I can usually find energy if there’s enough urgency or if something grabs me. It’s just that some tasks feel like they take a ridiculous amount of effort to start and stay with, and I can sit there sort of circling the work before actually doing it.\n\nSo… maybe a little, depending on what you mean, but I don’t think it feels like low drive exactly. More like friction. Like my brain doesn’t want to lock onto the thing unless there’s pressure or momentum.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel like your thoughts are foggy, it's hard to make decisions, or you can't think clearly even when you're not distracted by work mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it foggy exactly. I mean, I have moments where I’m trying to think through something and it feels like there are too many little pieces moving around, and then I kind of get stuck choosing where to start or what order to do things in. But that feels more like I’m scattered or I’m overcomplicating it, not like my brain is cloudy or I can’t think clearly.\n\nDecision-making can be annoying for me sometimes, especially if it’s a bunch of small choices that all seem equally important, but I usually can make the decision once I force myself to slow down. It’s not like I have whole days where I’m just mentally blank or confused.\n\nAnd if I’m not distracted by a specific mistake or something stressful, I generally feel like I can think fine. Maybe not always in the most organized way, but clear enough. So… no, I haven’t really noticed days of fogginess like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you don’t feel motivated to do things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about work or mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that specific way. I mean, I definitely have times where getting myself to start anything feels weirdly hard, even if it’s something I know I usually like. But once I actually start, or once I’m kind of pulled into it, I can still enjoy it.\n\nSo if you mean, like, I wake up and nothing sounds appealing all day and I don’t want to do any of the things I normally enjoy, even with work stuff not on my mind… I don’t really recognize that. It’s more that I might procrastinate on even fun things, or I’ll bounce around and not settle on what to do, and then later I’m annoyed because I wasted the free time.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like the motivation is gone because I’m emotionally flat or because I don’t care about anything. It’s more like I have trouble shifting into the activity. If someone else gets it started, or there’s a plan already, I’m usually fine. So no, I haven’t really noticed whole days like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time where your mood was unusually high, irritable, or you felt extremely energetic, even if others around you didn't seem to share that mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I definitely have days where I’m more revved up than other days, or I’m talking a lot, or I feel like I have a bunch of momentum and I’m jumping from thing to thing. But I don’t know that I’d call it “unusually high” in the way you might mean, like a mood state that feels really different from me or that other people are reacting to like, “What is going on with you?”\n\nI can get irritable, sure, but usually there’s some reason I can point to, or it’s more like I’m impatient because things feel too slow or I’m trying to get something done. I don’t think I’ve had stretches where I’m just intensely irritable or euphoric out of nowhere and everyone else is on a totally different wavelength.\n\nAnd “extremely energetic” is a little hard for me to answer because I can have bursts of energy, especially if something is interesting or there’s a lot happening. But it doesn’t feel like this separate period where my mood is elevated or I’m on top of the world or anything. It’s more just… my normal level can run kind of high sometimes, if that makes sense. So no, I haven’t really noticed periods like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you just didn’t feel like doing anything, even things you usually enjoy, and nothing really seemed to matter or give you pleasure?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had something like that for several days in a row. I’m trying to think of it carefully, because I do have times where I’m kind of useless about starting things, or I’ll sit around and waste an evening even though there are things I normally like doing. But it doesn’t feel like nothing matters, or like I can’t get pleasure from anything.\n\nUsually it’s more like I can’t decide what I want to do, or I’m restless and keep changing my mind, and then I end up not really doing anything satisfying. But if I actually get into something I enjoy, I still enjoy it. I don’t think I’ve had a stretch where everything felt pointless or flat for days. That doesn’t sound familiar to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Not really, no. I mean, I do know what you’re describing — like if I’m watching something beautifully shot or I’m working on an edit and normally I’d get that little, I don’t know, click of satisfaction when a cut lands right or the pacing starts working. I still get that.

But when I actually am engaged with it, I don’t feel like the pleasure has disappeared or gone dull compared to before.

So I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that things I usually enjoy feel empty or unsatisfying now. It’s more about getting myself settled into the thing, not that the enjoyment isn’t there.

I don’t think so—not in a clear way that I’ve noticed. I mean, I definitely have days where I don’t end up doing the enjoyable thing, or I kind of bounce around between options and then nothing really lands, but that feels different from not being able to take pleasure in it.
